# Session CC-20260812-k4m9

**Date:** 2026-09-02
**Branch:** `workstream/explorer-growth-epic11` (cut from `origin/main`)
**Scope:** EPIC 11 Phase A — the Explorer Growth Command Center's read backend. Seven epics
have been scoring, classifying and deciding on 153 learners every night, and the only way
anyone has seen any of it is me running SQL against production by hand. This makes it
readable over HTTP.

- [x] T000 — Classify the Command Center prefix in the RBAC gate
  - Date: 2026-09-02
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: One row in `mgmtSectionGate.ts` mapping `/api/admin/explorer-growth` to `campaigns`, the section the spec itself assigns the page (`EXPLORER_GROWTH_OS_PLAN.md:1381`). **The row grants nobody new access, and that is stated rather than glossed.** An earlier draft of the plan claimed it would admit the scoped management roles; it does not — `mgmtRoles.ts` gives curriculum `['dashboard','program']`, revenue `['dashboard','revenue','leads']`, admissions `['dashboard','lead_ingestion']`, support `['students']`, mentor `['dashboard','career_review']`, community_organizer `['dashboard']`, and **not one holds `campaigns`**. Only `owner` (which short-circuits before the map) and mgmt-`admin` reach it. The row earns its place because an unmapped path is a latent 403: the day someone grants `campaigns` to a scoped role, an unclassified path would deny them for a reason nothing in the code explains.
  - Verification: 28 tests. Every Command Center path classifies as `campaigns` (12 in-scope routes with concrete UUIDs, the bare prefix, EPIC 12's two routes, and Phase B's write paths); the segment boundary holds so `/api/admin/explorer-growth-legacy` returns null; and **exactly which roles hold `campaigns` is pinned** — owner and admin, with each of the six scoped roles asserted individually as not holding it, so granting it later is a visible diff rather than a silent widening of who can read learner data. **Mutation-checked:** removing the row fails 17 assertions while the role-pinning tests correctly still pass. Graded 12/12 by an independent verifier that rebuilt `pathToSection()` from the source text and reproduced the mutation without touching the repo.
  - Files: `backend/src/middlewares/mgmtSectionGate.ts`, `backend/src/middlewares/__tests__/mgmtSectionGate.explorerGrowth.test.ts`

- [x] T001 — Zod contracts that reject rather than repair
  - Date: 2026-09-02
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `schemas/explorerGrowthSchema.ts` — every input the twelve read routes accept. `z.coerce` appears nowhere: query parameters genuinely arrive as strings, so they are parsed explicitly with a digits-only regex and then a real numeric bound. `limit=500` is a 400, **not a clamp to 50** — a caller who asks for 500 and receives 50 rows was answered a question they did not ask, and nothing in the response says so. `2026-02-30` passes the shape check and is rejected by a UTC round-trip. `"false"` parses to `false` rather than truthy. Every query object is `.strict()`, so a typo'd filter 400s instead of silently returning an unfiltered list that looks filtered.
  - Verification: 92 tests. The enum arrays are pinned to the type unions in `types/explorerGrowth.ts` in **both** directions — `as const satisfies` catches an extra member, an `Exhaustive<>` conditional-type alias catches a missing one — and both were mutation-tested (`TS2322` each way). **Those guards live in the source file deliberately**: `tsconfig` excludes `__tests__/` and ts-jest transpiles without type-checking, so the same guard in a test file would be checked by nothing, a mistake this workstream already made once. Graded 12/12; the verifier probed the numeric parser with Arabic-Indic digits, hex, `Infinity` and a prototype-pollution payload (all rejected) and ran `isoDaySchema` in seven timezones from UTC-12 to UTC+14 with identical results.

- [x] T002 — The Why drilldown: every losing candidate, with the reason it lost
  - Date: 2026-09-02
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `explorerWhyService.ts` (+ `explorerWhyTypes.ts`, split out when the service crossed the 300-line ceiling). For one learner on one day it assembles the decision, the scores it was made on, **every suppressed candidate with its reason**, the content or the named gap, contactability, and a `drift` block. Nothing is recomputed — re-running the scorer would answer "what would we decide now", which diverges from "what did we decide and why" the moment any score moves, and a nightly recompute moves them constantly. `scores_at_decision` reads the decision row; `scores_now` reads the profile; both are returned separately labelled. A learner with no decision gets a stated absence across four distinguished cases, never an empty object.
  - Verification: 31 tests against a fixture copied **verbatim from a real production row** (decision `771952ba-…`), because EPIC 1 shipped a bug that passed eleven green tests on a mock encoding an assumption rather than the driver's behaviour. Graded 11/12 then remediated: the verifier transpiled the real service and ran it against **all 153 production rows** — winner named 153/153, `suppressed` byte-identical 153/153, gaps parsed 12/12, zero empty objects. **Verifying against real data changed the implementation twice.** First, the Governor already writes a *named* gap into `reason` (`asset gaps: no_asset_for_purpose:lesson_recommendation:learning`), so the payload surfaces that instead of my weaker derived sentence. Second, the gap token joins its stages with a bare `|` while reason segments join with `' | '` — splitting on the bare character silently truncated a multi-stage gap on the one surface built to report gaps faithfully.
  - Notes: `getExplorerWhy` was 125 lines against this plan's own ≤50 rule; extracted to five helpers, none over 35.
  - Files: `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/explorerWhyService.ts`, `explorerWhyTypes.ts`, `explorerGapParsing.ts`, `__tests__/explorerWhyService.test.ts`

- [x] T003 — The six tab queries, written after the queries were run
  - Date: 2026-09-02
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `explorerOverviewService.ts` (Overview, Journey, the mode Settings shows) and `explorerDecisionsService.ts` (Decisions, Shadow, Content). Every query was executed against production **before the code was written**, because "complete plumbing carrying no water" is this programme's signature failure. Counts anchor on `MAX(decision_date)` rather than `CURRENT_DATE`: the recompute is nightly, so "today" returns zeroes on a morning before it has run, which reads as "the system decided nothing" rather than "it has not run yet".
  - Verification: 36 tests, and **graded 6/12 FAIL on the first attempt for a defect that would have 500'd the Content tab in production.** Sequelize's `replacements` is textual substitution, not a server-side bind, so `kinds: ['LESSON']` rendered as `CAST('LESSON' AS text[])` — `ERROR: malformed array literal`. All four supported purposes would have rejected at once through `Promise.all`, taking the matrix and gap report down with them. The fix uses `pgArray` **exported** from `content/resolveContentAssets.ts` rather than copied. Proven by rendering through `injectReplacements` with the real dialect and piping the result into production psql: broken form errors, fixed form returns `free_preview 23 / full_access 552` and `23 / 47`. A new test asserts the bound **value** is an array literal — the class of check that was missing entirely, since all 33 tests mocked `sequelize.query` and asserted on the SQL *template*, which is structurally blind to a binding bug. Re-graded 12/12.
  - Notes: **The Content tab reports a real finding.** All 206 learning-stage assets are `full_access`; the only free-preview content in the catalogue is 23 activation assets. A free-tier learner past week 0 therefore has an empty candidate set *by construction* — which is why the tab returns the stage×audience matrix alongside the count rather than instead of it. That is an editorial decision for Ali about what the free tier should see, not a bug. I also nearly computed "coverage by purpose" from `asset_type`, which has exactly one distinct value (`LESSON`); purposes resolve through `PURPOSE_SPECS` and a kinds/stage/tier predicate.
  - Files: `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/explorerOverviewService.ts`, `explorerDecisionsService.ts`, `content/resolveContentAssets.ts`, `__tests__/explorerOverviewService.test.ts`, `__tests__/explorerDecisionsService.test.ts`

- [x] T004 — Twelve read routes, a thin controller, and a failure path that does not kill the process
  - Date: 2026-09-02
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `routes/admin/explorerGrowthRoutes.ts` (12 GETs, path-scoped `requireAdmin`), `controllers/explorerGrowthController.ts`, and `explorerLearnerService.ts` for the five learner-drawer routes no tab query covered. Registered in `adminRoutes.ts`. §27's other two GETs, `/forecast` and `/experiments`, are EPIC 12's and are **asserted absent** rather than stubbed.
  - Verification: 102 route tests + 20 learner-service tests; 308 across all seven suites under CI's own config; `lint-route-auth` OK across 105 admin route files. **Graded 9/12 FAIL on the first attempt for a defect that would have taken production down.** `handler()` awaited the service with no `try`/`catch`, and Express 4 does not catch async rejections: the rejection escapes to the process, no response is sent, and production runs Node's default `--unhandled-rejections=throw` with `listenerCount === 0` and `RestartPolicy=always`. One connection blip on an admin page nobody was watching would have restarted the whole backend container for every user. The repo had already written this lesson down at `db/ensureExplorerCampaignKeyIndex.ts:24` and the sibling `caseStudyMetricRoutes.ts` wraps every service call; I followed neither. Now wrapped, classified via the repo's own `classifyError`, logged as one structured line that never touches the payload, and returned as 500 — **tested across all twelve routes**, and mutation-checked (stripping the guard fails 15 tests). Re-graded 12/12.
  - Notes: **This epic surfaced a real gap in EPIC 4.** §27 describes `/learners/:id/signals` as a "signal timeline with weights + decay". That timeline does not exist: `explorer_journey_decisions.triggering_signals` is declared, defaults to `[]`, and **no code path writes it** — `runGovernor.ts` never references the column and 0 of 612 production rows are populated. The route returns the signal summary (populated on all 153 profiles) and the real E/I/F series (918 snapshots), plus `per_signal_timeline_available: false` with a stated reason. Returning `signals: []` would have read as "this learner produced no signals" about learners whose scores demonstrably moved. **Worth a follow-up ticket against the Governor.** Also chased a flaky auth assertion to its cause rather than re-running it: `requireAdmin`'s 401 path does a dynamic `import()` of a service that pulls in the model layer, costing 875ms on first failure; mocking it (as the sibling route test does) dropped it to 32ms.
  - Files: `backend/src/routes/admin/explorerGrowthRoutes.ts`, `backend/src/controllers/explorerGrowthController.ts`, `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/explorerLearnerService.ts`, `backend/src/routes/adminRoutes.ts`, `backend/src/routes/admin/__tests__/explorerGrowthRoutes.access.test.ts`, `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/__tests__/explorerLearnerService.test.ts`

## Phase B — the page

- [x] B000 - The typed client, and a baseline that immediately earned itself
  - Date: 2026-09-03
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `frontend/src/services/explorerGrowthApi.ts` - one typed function per Phase A endpoint, twelve in all, with types mirroring the backend schemas by hand (no shared-types package exists; `capeApi.ts` documents that as the convention). **The client has no write method at all.** Phase A shipped no write route, and §27's seven are a governance decision rather than a build task, so a client that cannot call one is a stronger guarantee than a client that merely does not.
  - Verification: 25 tests. Every call is checked for its path AND for the query parameters it actually forwards - the second being the half that fails silently, because a client that drops a filter returns a plausible unfiltered list while the page looks like the filter worked. Also asserted: `executed: false` survives (a truthiness check would drop it and show a reviewer the executed decisions they were filtering out), unset filters are omitted rather than sent as the string "undefined" (which the backend's `.strict()` schemas would 400), and no mutating verb is ever called.
  - Notes: **The pinned `tsc` baseline caught a regression within minutes.** After this file landed, `tsc` went 0 to 2 - and because the baseline was a specific number rather than "some pre-existing noise", both were immediately identifiable as mine: TypeScript interfaces carry no implicit index signature, so `LearnersQuery` was not assignable to `Record<string, unknown>`. A generic constraint fixed it without loosening a type. The baseline had also already proved the plan-audit's point about itself: it moved 10 to 0 with no source change, because an `npx` invocation triggered an install that pulled in the previously-missing `@dnd-kit`.
  - Files: `frontend/src/services/explorerGrowthApi.ts`, `frontend/src/services/__tests__/explorerGrowthApi.test.ts`

- [x] B001-B005 - The page: six tabs, the Why drilldown, and a gate proven on the real defect
  - Date: 2026-09-03
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `/admin/explorer-growth`, built entirely from the existing admin shell (`PageHeader`, `StatCard`, `StatusBadge`, `SectionCard`), Bootstrap 5 and RemixIcon, no new design system and no literal hex. Six tabs - Overview, Journey, Decisions, Shadow, Content, Settings - plus `DecisionWhyModal`, `LearnerDrawer` and `SignalTimeline`. Route registered lazily like every sibling; nav entry in the Campaigns group under `section: 'campaigns'`, which is the same section the **backend** gate already classifies `/api/admin/explorer-growth` under, so nav visibility and API access agree by construction rather than by luck. The trust badge is derived from the response - `live` with the real decision date, `error` with the actual message, `unverified` while unknown - never a hardcoded `verified`, which would be a lie told by the one component whose job is trustworthiness.
  - Verification: 63 tests across four suites; `tsc` at its pinned baseline of 0 from the 5.9.3 binary; `CI=true npm run build:frontend` `Compiled successfully`, exit 0. **Both load-bearing guards mutation-proven:** deleting the nav entry fails 6 assertions (where the pre-existing `adminNavRbac.test.ts` passes unchanged - a plan audit caught me crediting it, and it proves the filter mechanism rather than that this link is subject to it); adding `.slice(0, 3)` to the losers fails exactly one test, the nine-loser case, because the two-loser cases pass straight through a three-item cap. That asymmetry is the whole reason the nine-loser case exists. Mechanical sweep clean on all five contract non-negotiables: no write path, no literal hex, no `bi-` name reaching a shell `icon` prop (the shell interpolates `ri-${icon}`, so one would render nothing), no `console.*` at all, no live hooks-rule suppression.
  - Notes: **I wrote the documented 2026-08-15 break into this diff and used it to prove the gate.** An `eslint-disable react-hooks/exhaustive-deps` in a `.ts` file - a rule CRA does not register for `.ts`, making the comment itself an ESLint error that `CI=true` turns fatal while `tsc` stays silent. I caught it while writing, and before fixing it ran the build deliberately: `Failed to compile`, exit 1, with `tsc` reporting 0 errors on the same code. That is the evidence for switching the gate from `eslint` (which **cannot run** in this repo - no config exists anywhere, and my first "clean baseline" from it was a false green read out of a failed command) to `CI=true npm run build:frontend`, the only command here that applies the react-app ruleset. The fix removed the reason for the suppression rather than relocating it: the hook takes a stable string key instead of a spread dependency array. Two other decisions worth recording - `AsyncPanel` makes "loading != empty != error" a component rather than a convention, because a rule applied by hand on six tabs gets applied correctly on five; and `@testing-library/react` was NOT added despite 14 files importing it and `package.json` declaring it nowhere, since a drive-by install is forbidden and `renderToStaticMarkup` (198 uses here, and what the spec's file map named) forced the better shape of testing pure exported render bodies.
  - Files: `frontend/src/pages/admin/ExplorerGrowthPage.tsx`, `frontend/src/components/explorerGrowth/*` (12 files), `frontend/src/components/explorerGrowth/__tests__/DecisionWhyModal.test.tsx`, `.../ContentTab.test.tsx`, `frontend/src/__tests__/adminNavExplorerGrowth.test.ts`, `frontend/src/routes/adminRoutes.tsx`, `frontend/src/components/Layout/adminNav.ts`

### Deliberately out of scope, declared rather than omitted

§26's mockup puts `[ SHADOW ▾ ] [ Recalculate ] [ ⛔ PAUSE ]` in the page header. None
were built, for two independent reasons, and the Settings tab states both on the page so
nobody waits for a panel that is not coming:

1. **All seven of §27's write routes are unbuilt.** The buttons would 404, and a kill
   switch that looks clickable but does nothing is worse than none.
2. **The mode control is a governance boundary, not a widget.** Its upper values
   (`pilot`, `limited`, `full`) are what would let this system begin sending email to 153
   real learners, against a standing "nothing sends" constraint. Under CLAUDE.md's
   Autonomy Model that makes it an escalation, not a task - shipping it inside "build the
   page" would have been the governance violation, not deferring it.

## Reassessment, 2026-09-06

- [x] Read the enrolment page as intent, and correct two findings I got wrong
  - Date: 2026-09-06
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: One CASE arm in `explorerSignalReader.ts` - `page_category = 'enroll'` now maps to `enrollment_cta_click`. Measured over seven days on production, `enroll` carried **196 events, 57 attributed to a person** - the best-attributed category in the table, and unread, while `pricing` (57 events, **0** attributed) and `program` (64 events, 2 attributed) were both mapped. Someone on an enrolment page is acting rather than browsing, so the clearest available signal was being discarded. Mapped to tier 2 deliberately: HIGH_INTENT needs tier 3+, so reaching an enrolment page must not on its own promote a learner into the overlay.
  - Verification: 24 tests (was 22). Mutation-checked - deleting the CASE arm fails exactly one. Type-clean.
  - Notes: **Two things in my 3 September review were wrong, and this change exists mostly to correct them.** (1) I said the system had been "deciding nightly for weeks". It has not: `EXPLORER_GROWTH_OS_ENABLED` is absent from the production `.env` and from compose, so the master switch has never been on in production. The five decision runs (08-23 to 09-02) were manual, during development. (2) I said the event names did not match what the scorer reads. **They do** - the reader already translates `cta_click` to `enrollment_cta_click` and `form_start` to `enrollment_form_started`. I had compared the tracker's output against the internal signal vocabulary rather than against the reader's own query. I also proposed building visitor-to-person identity resolution; `visitorTrackingService.resolveIdentity` already does it, including an idempotent backfill of past `page_events` and sessions. **Two of my three recommendations were already built.**
  - Notes (what the reassessment did find): tracking genuinely improved - event types 2 to 12, CTA clicks 0 to 335, form starts 0 to 57, attributed rows 34 to 235, plus a real fix where sendBeacon posted `text/plain` and express discarded the payload. But intent still reads zero for the 153 largely because **85 of them have never been active at all** and only 10 were active this week. There is little to measure, and that dormancy is the condition the activation campaigns exist to treat. **51 mappable intent signals already sit in `page_events` for identified leads, unread, because the engine has never run.**
  - Files: `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/explorerSignalReader.ts`, `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/__tests__/explorerSignalReader.test.ts`

- [x] EPIC 5 safety layer: strict template renderer + the April-14 copy lint

Single-pass by design as a security property: a learner whose display name is `{{secret.key}}` gets that string printed back, where a second pass would make any learner-controlled field a read primitive over the whole context. Own-properties-only, so `{{constructor.name}}` and `{{__proto__.x}}` do not resolve. Scanning every string literal took it to **77**. Two further real defects surfaced from running it against actual copy rather than fixtures: it flagged `NEVER_STATE_CLAUSE` - the guard instructing the model *not* to state a deadline - so a prohibition is now distinguished from an assertion; and that clause is split across concatenated literals, so the negation sat in fragment one and "deadline" in fragment two, making the verdict depend on where the line happened to wrap. Adjacent literals are now joined before matching.
  - Notes (CI): the `guards` job keeps its stale name **deliberately**. "Secret scan + route-auth lint" is a REQUIRED status check on main (verified via the protection API); renaming the job renames the context, and main would then wait forever on a check that no longer reports. A comment in `ci.yml` says so.

- [x] EPIC 12: cohort forecast + experiment assignment
  - Date: 2026-09-07
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `explorerForecastService.ts` (§24 model: 8 funnel stages, Wilson score intervals, n>=30 and window>=30d gates) and `explorerExperimentService.ts` (§25: stable FNV-1a assignment, lift with a Wilson interval, refused below n=100 per arm). Both pure - no I/O - so both are exhaustively testable, which matters because their output is a number a human plans against.
  - Verification: 42 tests across 2 suites, `tsc --noEmit` exit 0. Wilson checked against the published interval for n=100/p=0.5 ([0.4038, 0.5962]). **Mutation-checked on the propagation rule**: making an unknown stage rate be skipped - the natural shortcut, equivalent to treating it as 1.0 - fails 5 of 23 forecast tests.
  - Notes (the forecast): §24.1 forbids fabricating a benchmark, so **one unknown rate makes the WHOLE projection unknown**. Every stage's contribution passes through every downstream rate, so a single gap breaks every term; computing from the stages that happen to have data would present partial coverage as a total. `Measured` is a discriminated union rather than `number | null` specifically so "we do not know" cannot be coerced by `?? 0` or `|| 1`. The gap interval is INVERTED - the low total implies the largest shortfall - because carrying bounds straight through would report the optimistic case as the worst case.
  - Notes (the ethics rule, §25.2): **the holdout list is an ALLOWLIST.** No learner may be withheld from a message addressing a problem they are actually having - friction recovery, payment failure, support, opt-out, event logistics, security. `assignArm` cannot return `control` for those; it returns treatment WITH a reason rather than throwing, because a throw makes the safe path the one that breaks the cron, and a broken nightly job gets "fixed" under pressure by removing the check. An unknown intervention is ineligible by default, so the cost of forgetting to classify a new type is a lost experiment rather than a learner left without help. Tested across all 500 sample learners rather than a sample, since the guarantee is absolute.
  - Notes (assignment): the experiment key is part of the hash so a learner's position is independent per experiment. Hashing the learner alone would put the same people in control every time and silently make their traits the definition of the control group system-wide.
  - Files: `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/explorerForecastService.ts`, `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/explorerExperimentService.ts`, and their two test files

- [x] EPIC 13: health checks, built around the failure that hid all day
  - Date: 2026-09-07
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `explorerGrowthHealthService.ts` - four pure evaluators (coverage, staleness per §8.3's 26h rule, decision volume, suppression rate) plus a report whose severity is its worst check. Pure by design so the judgment, which is the part that is easy to get subtly wrong, is testable without a database.
  - Verification: 25 tests, `tsc --noEmit` exit 0. One test is fed **live production numbers read off `accelerator_prod` today** (213 active Explorers / 152 profiled / 153 profiles older than 26h) and asserts the report comes back critical on both coverage and staleness.
  - Notes: **The coverage check exists because of this morning's incident, and staleness could never have found it.** The recompute iterated the profiles table, so it only refreshed learners who already had a row - 212 active, 152 profiled, 60 invisible, and the batch reported `succeeded: 152, failed: 0`. Nothing was stale, nothing errored, every metric was green, because *a staleness check reads the profiles table and a learner with no row is not in it*. The general lesson, written into the file header: **a check that counts the population it is checking will never find the population it is missing.** So coverage counts against ENROLLMENTS and asks who *should* have a profile and does not. There is a test asserting that 152 profiles all recomputed on time reads as perfectly healthy under staleness alone.
  - Notes (thresholds): coverage is CRITICAL at a single missing learner, not only at scale - a missing profile is not a degraded signal, that learner is absent from every roster, forecast stage and decision. Decision volume is critical in BOTH directions, since on a send-enabled path 3x normal is the shape of an incident, and zero reads as calm on a chart while being the loudest signal there is. A HIGH suppression rate is healthy (WAIT is by design the most common outcome); 100% and 0% are the faults.
  - Notes (measured today, worth acting on): the active-Explorer count moved 212 → 213 during the session. One new Explorer signed up and was born invisible, which is the growing-gap argument in a single number.
  - Files: `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/explorerGrowthHealthService.ts`, `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/__tests__/explorerGrowthHealthService.test.ts`

- [x] EPIC 8 (part 1): inbound reply classifier, with opt-out kept away from the model
  - Date: 2026-09-07
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `explorerReplyClassifier.ts` - the 12 §15.5 classes, §15.5 routing, and deterministic opt-out detection. Pure, with the model call injected, so every routing rule is testable without a network.
  - Verification: 44 tests, `tsc --noEmit` exit 0.
  - Notes: **The tests caught a real defect in my own opt-out patterns, of exactly the class the module exists to prevent.** `\b(remove|take)\s+me\s+(off|from)\b` matched "Remove me from the **waitlist**, I want the full cohort" and "Can you take me off **mute** in the session?" - two people asking for something ordinary, both of whom would have been permanently and silently suppressed. The pattern now requires an actual contact channel to be named. `\blist\b` does not match inside "waitlist", and that word boundary is the whole difference between a mailing list and a waiting list.
  - Notes (why opt-out is model-free, both directions): §15.5 says deterministic only. Missing an opt-out means continuing to email someone who asked us to stop. Inventing one is not harmless either - `processOptOut` cascades to `Lead.status`, every `CampaignLead` lifecycle, pending `ScheduledEmail`s, a consent revoke and a GHL DND tag, and §35 D-4 says opt-outs are **never retroactively reversed**. Nobody notices a message that was not sent. So deterministic patterns decide and the model cannot override them either way; a model-only OPT_OUT becomes a HUMAN_TASK rather than being acted on or discarded.
  - Notes (quoted history): the detector strips `>` quoted lines first. Our own unsubscribe footer is on every message we send, so without that, replying in a thread opts the replier out.
  - Notes (route type): there is deliberately no `AUTO_REPLY` route. §15.5 permits auto-reply only later and behind its own flag, and a value that exists in the type is a value someone eventually returns.
  - Files: `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/explorerReplyClassifier.ts`, `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/__tests__/explorerReplyClassifier.test.ts`

Registration input gains `entrySite`; the Zod schema accepts it.

All 6 production organizations are `enterprise_customer`, and the vocabulary the model documents is `enterprise_customer | community_partner | church | nonprofit_partner | client | internal | sponsor`. Defaulting to `management_account`, as that migration implies, would have created a seventh value no row uses and no query looks for - the taxonomy split this module exists to prevent.

Routing registrations through it would enrol every new business account into lead nurture - real sends, to someone who just registered and is already receiving the welcome email. More importantly, Ali said **Colaberry Enterprise will later have the same consulting functionality**, and the hostname-only table I first shipped **could never express that**: `enterprise.colaberry.ai` is already the Business and Training entrance, so the domain would have to be one or the other for everyone. The table now accepts `host` OR `host/path` keys with **longest-match-wins**, so adding `enterprise.colaberry.ai/consulting` when that page exists is one line rather than a redesign.

  - Date: 2026-09-08
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `explorerVoiceEligibility.ts` (all 14 §16.2 conditions) and `explorerTimezoneService.ts` (resolve-or-refuse, plus the learner-local call window). Both pure - "would we have called this person" is answerable without a database, and the gate is testable with no possibility of placing a call.
  - Verification: 54 tests, `tsc --noEmit` exit 0. Per-condition fail-closed coverage as §16.2 requires, not aggregate: 20 separate cases each turning exactly one condition bad. Plus a test that an entirely empty context refuses.
  - Notes (**measured against production today**): **zero learners could pass this gate.** No Explorer has reached `HIGH_INTENT`, so the intent condition alone blocks all 214 - before the flag, before consent, before anything. 4 `consent_records` do exist with `channel='voice'`, `status='granted'` and an express basis, so consent is not the only thing holding it shut. The gate is unreachable on current data as well as flag-off.
  - Notes (why voice is the strict one): every other channel's worst case is an unwanted email. This one's is an unconsented automated call - a TCPA claim per call and a real intrusion into someone's day. So: tier 4 not tier 3, F < 15 not F < 25, and consent limited to `express_written` or `double_opt_in`. An implied or legitimate-interest basis satisfies email and does not satisfy a robocall; there is a test per rejected basis.
  - Notes (§35 D-3, the timezone refusal): there is no per-lead timezone, and calling someone at a locally-inappropriate hour is the exposure. The resolution table is deliberately SMALL - only regions wholly inside one zone. FL, TX, KS, NE, ND, SD, TN, KY, IN, MI, OR, ID and AK are **excluded on purpose**: a state-level guess for a split state is a coin flip, and a learner in west Texas is an hour off one in Houston. A narrow table that is right beats a broad one that is right most of the time, because "most of the time" is measured in calls to real people at hours they never agreed to. DST comes from `Intl` against the zone rather than a stored offset - an offset captured once is wrong for half the year, in the direction of calling an hour early.
  - Notes (**still blocked, and not by code**): plan §35 D-1 says voice and SMS **cannot ship** until consent enforcement moves from `shadow` to `enforce` - a decision awaiting Ali since 2026-06-22. §16 also states "Ships disabled. `EXPLORER_AUTO_DIAL_ENABLED=false`. Not enabled without the §35 compliance decisions." Nothing here changes that.
  - Notes (**CI caught what my scoped local runs could not**): the branch failed `CI / Backend unit tests` on `config/__tests__/explorerGrowthFlags.test.ts` - the guard that scans the whole backend for `.<subFlag>` property reads to catch code bypassing the master switch. My context interface field was named `autoDialEnabled`, which is **exactly** the flags-object property name. It was a caller-supplied boolean, not a flag read, but the scanner cannot tell the difference and is right not to try - its own comment says a match outside the flags module is "either the bypass we are preventing or a name collision worth knowing about". Renamed to `autoDialFlagOn`, with the reason recorded at the field.
  - Notes (why it passed locally and failed in CI): running the suite **on the branch alone** passed. CI tests the branch MERGED WITH MAIN, and I had only ever run the voice test file by path. This is the documented trap - CI is `jest -c jest.ci.config.ts` over everything, and a scoped local run misses contract tests entirely. Reproduced it locally only after merging `origin/main` in.

  - Date: 2026-09-08

  - What changed: `channelSuppression.ts` (pure) plus wiring into the two places that enforce suppression. `processOptOut` no longer sets the **global** `Lead.status='unsubscribed'` and blanket `CampaignLead.lifecycle_status='dnd'` for an **sms or voice** opt-out; `checkLeadSendable` gained an optional `channel` and now evaluates unsubscribe events per channel.
  - Verification: 27 tests in `channelSuppression.test.ts`, `tsc --noEmit` exit 0, plus the **full** `jest -c jest.ci.config.ts` suite rather than a scoped run - this is a shared send-safety chokepoint and the earlier lesson today was that scoped local runs miss contract tests.
  - Notes (**a second global block nobody had noticed**): the documented defect was `processOptOut` suppressing globally. But `checkLeadSendable` also blocked on the mere EXISTENCE of any `unsubscribe_events` row - ignoring the `channel` column entirely, despite the table carrying a `(lead_id, channel)` index the whole time. Either one alone would have kept an SMS STOP killing email, so fixing only the documented half would have produced a green change with no behavioural effect.
  - Notes (**the cutoff is the safety property, not a detail**): rows written before `PER_CHANNEL_SUPPRESSION_CUTOFF` are still read as GLOBAL. Someone who texted STOP in June has `channel='sms'` and has been treated as fully unsubscribed ever since; reading those per-channel would silently make them emailable again - re-opening a door they closed, at scale, without anyone asking. §35 D-4 is explicit: "only for opt-outs recorded after the change. Never retroactively un-suppress anyone." An undateable row is treated as legacy for the same reason.
  - Notes (the old query read only the newest row): harmless while any row blocked everything, but once channel matters, "the latest one was sms" could hide an older global opt-out behind it. Now reads all rows for the lead.
  - Notes (backward compatibility): `checkLeadSendable(leadId)` with no channel behaves **exactly** as before - any event blocks. Every existing caller is in that position, so no unreviewed send path changes today; callers opt in by naming a channel. Email and any unrecognised channel value keep today's global behaviour, since an email unsubscribe IS the primary meaning of `Lead.status='unsubscribed'`.
  - Notes (**scoping, checked not assumed**): the `training-site` skill warns most consent work spans both repos. It does not here. The training site's `src/lib/training/consent.ts` **captures** marketing consent at signup and forwards it to enterprise - it writes no consent records and has no opt-out or STOP handling at all. Enterprise-only change, verified by reading that file rather than assuming.
  - Files: `backend/src/services/channelSuppression.ts`, `backend/src/services/communicationSafetyService.ts`, `backend/src/services/unsubscribeEnforcementService.ts`, `backend/src/services/__tests__/channelSuppression.test.ts`

- [x] Explorer fact resolver: give the send-time guard something to check against
  - Date: 2026-09-09
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `explorerFactResolver.ts` (pure) resolves the dates, prices and seat counts a message may state (§11.3), and `contextGraphService.buildCompositeContext` now populates `context.explorer` from it. This closes the producer-without-consumer gap left when the fact guard shipped: with nothing populating the set, the guard failed closed and **any** copy naming a fact was refused - correct, and useless.
  - Verification: 21 tests, `tsc --noEmit` exit 0, full `jest -c jest.ci.config.ts`. The end-to-end test is the one that matters: resolver + guard together accept "November 12" (the real intake) and still reject "April 14".
  - Notes (**it will not name a cohort that has already started**): `selectNextOpenCohort` falls back to an already-started cohort, then to the most recently created one. That is right for PLACEMENT - a signup has to land somewhere - and wrong for a sentence in an email. That fallback is how "the upcoming April 14 cohort" survived five months. `nextQuotableCohort` returns null instead, and §11.3 says no cohort means the message is cancelled.
  - Notes (**three traps found in live cohort data, not in fixtures**): July 2026 has **50 seats and 58 taken**, so a naive `max - taken` quotes "-8 seats remaining" - omitted. "Explorer — Prospects" carries **100,000 seats** and is a holding pen for Open House signups, not an intake; "99,998 seats remaining" is not a sentence to send anyone, so implausible seat counts are skipped. And "Cohort — April 2026" is still there, completed, `start_date 2026-04-14` - the cohort behind the stale line removed yesterday. The test fixtures are these four real rows.
  - Notes (dates parsed as UTC parts, not `new Date()`): a `YYYY-MM-DD` string parsed in a negative-offset timezone lands on the previous day, which would authorise the wrong date - off by one, in the direction of telling someone class starts a day early.
  - Notes (**scope of the wiring**): `context.explorer` is populated ONLY for leads with an Explorer journey profile. Its presence is what turns the guard on, so populating it for every lead would switch fact-checking on for every campaign at once and existing copy naming a figure would start failing to send. Absent = not an Explorer, guard off. Empty = Explorer with no quotable facts, the strictest state.
  - Notes (prices): from `subscriptionService.PLANS`, excluding `comp` - an admin-granted $0 seat never listed for self-serve checkout, and quoting $0 to a prospect would be false. Both the term price and `per_month` are resolved, since $149/year genuinely is $12.42/month.
  - Files: `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/explorerFactResolver.ts`, `backend/src/services/explorerGrowth/__tests__/explorerFactResolver.test.ts`, `backend/src/services/contextGraphService.ts`

Registering beside the Explorer step, which is the obvious place, would reference tables that do not exist yet. Because each statement is individually try/caught, that surfaces as a **console warning at boot, not a crash**: the tables would simply be absent until something queried them. Two tests assert the registration order.

A TypeScript union still exists so a typo fails at the call site: the database stays permissive, the code stays strict.

- [x] T202: offer families, brand-offer policy, and the eligibility gate that reads them
  - Date: 2026-09-10
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `offer_families` (§4's governed catalog) and `brand_offer_policies` (§4's eligibility mapping, all eight §4:278 attributes) added to the same additive ensure module; models for both; §4's required policy as data; an idempotent seed wired into boot; and `services/growthJourney/offerEligibility.ts`, the server-side gate that resolves brand x offer.

  - Notes (**the resolver was not in the plan's file list, and building it anyway was the call**): T202's acceptance criteria say a contract test proves `ai-flotation` "resolves" no denied family. Resolution needs a resolver; without one the test would query rows directly and the policy table would be a **producer with no consumer** - the exact failure this programme has now paid for repeatedly. §4 also states the requirement in enforcement terms: "Every content lookup, candidate action, AI prompt, landing-page suggestion and campaign enrollment must enforce brand x offer eligibility server-side." Logged as a scope addition rather than a silent one.
  - Notes (**the three rules, and why each is a rule rather than a preference**): (1) **fail closed** - no row denies, an unrecognised family denies, and a **failed lookup denies**; there is no input and no upstream failure that yields allowed. (2) **deny outranks allow, unconditionally** - `status` and the effective window soften an allow, never a deny, so a deny cannot be lifted by pausing it or letting it expire, only by deleting the row. §4:287 requires AI Flotation to be safe "even when the classifier, content tags or caller request are wrong", and the realistic failure is somebody **adding** a grant, not forgetting one. (3) **eligibility is not content approval** - `allowed` says the brand may offer the family; `approved_content_ready` is returned separately so a content lookup cannot read one as the other.
  - Notes (**the acceptance criterion "deleting a deny row fails the test" is a vacuity trap, and I nearly walked into it**): because absence already denies, a test asserting only `allowed === false` would **still pass** with the deny row deleted - default-deny would produce the same answer, and the mutation check would certify nothing. The tests assert the **reason** (`explicit_deny` vs `no_policy`), which is what makes the deny rows load-bearing. Confirmed by running it: MUT-1 deleting the whole deny entry gave 9 failures; MUT-2 dropping just `learner_internship` from the six gave 8.
  - Notes (three more mutations, on the resolver): MUT-3 letting a paused deny stop denying - fails; MUT-4 opening the gate when the lookup throws - fails; MUT-5 removing the catalog check on the caller's input - 3 failures. Every source restored byte-identical after each.
  - Notes (**the seed's asymmetry is deliberate and tested both ways**): following `seedExplorerGrowthCampaigns`, `status` and all six approved-content lists are **never written on update** - an operator's pause and their curated claims survive every boot. Against that, a `deny` **is** re-asserted: a §4:287 row flipped to `allow` is put back, the same way that seed re-asserts `is_active: false`, because where a value is the safety property rather than a preference, inheriting the existing state is how the safety property gets quietly lost. But an `allow` **never** overwrites an existing `deny` - a human who closed something the spec permits has made a decision, and auto-reopening it would be the override the seed is forbidden from doing. Deny is the safe direction, so only the safe direction is forced.
  - Notes (**the seed invents no approved content, and that is asserted**): §4 opens with "Offers are not free-text AI inventions." Every seeded row therefore ships its approved landing pages, claims, content collections, CTAs and conversion events **empty**, with `required_approvals: ['human_copy_review']`. A seeded URL or claim would be exactly what that sentence forbids, and worse, would arrive pre-labelled approved. A test greps the definitions for any URL and asserts none.
  - Notes (assumption logged - **scholarship**): §4's CPN row names "scholarship pathways" but §4's family list has no scholarship family. Rather than invent a twelfth (the plan pins the list at eleven), scholarship is carried as `learner_free_training`, with the framing belonging to the policy row's claims and CTAs that a human fills in. If it needs its own family later, that is an additive catalog row, not a migration.
  - Notes (CPN gets **two** families, not five): §4 says "learner/free-training and approved scholarship/community pathways **only**". The word only excludes paid training, certification and internship, which Colaberry Training does carry. AI Flotation's allowed set is **derived** as Enterprise minus `business_training` rather than typed out again, so a family added to Enterprise later cannot be silently withheld - or worse, copy-pasted with training included.
  - Notes (**the boot wiring is where this could have failed silently**): the seed writes to tables `ensureGrowthJourneySchema` creates. Seeding first is not a crash - every write is individually caught - it is a few warnings and an **empty policy table**, which then denies every brand every family. Safe, silent and wrong. A falsifiable test asserts the seed is registered after the ensure step.
  - Notes (two test bugs of mine, caught by running rather than reading): an escaped bracket pair inside a template literal collapses to a bare `[]`, making the pattern an **empty character class** that matches nothing - the assertion failed against DDL that was already correct. And one seed test mocked every row as `allow`, including the six denies, so the seed correctly restored them and the test read that correct behaviour as a failure.
  - Notes (live evidence, with the control that earned its keep): the first attempt ran `psql -f` on a path that existed on the **host**, not inside the container, so nothing executed - and the "snapshots identical" line reported a pass. The a-vs-b control caught it. On the real run: 0 tables before, created on pass 1, a-vs-b differ, b-vs-c `information_schema` and `pg_indexes` **identical**, both unique indexes present as control strings, live FKs to `brands` and `tenants`, 17 policy columns and 8 catalog columns matching the models, six JSONB columns defaulting to an empty array, and `explorer_%` unchanged at **86**.
  - Notes (duplicate list closed): T201's verifier flagged `LEARNER_OFFER_FAMILIES` as a future duplicate. The list **moved** to `models/OfferFamily.ts` rather than being copied, and `JourneyPath.ts` carries a comment saying why it is not even a re-export.
  - Notes (**the verifier's own mutation found a gap mine missed, and it was a claim I had written into a comment**): T202 graded **11/12 PASS**, and the deduction was the one thing worth deducting. `offerEligibility.ts` carries a comment saying the single-row and list paths "cannot disagree" - but `allowedOfferFamilies` is a second implementation of the same three rules, and hoisting its `status !== 'active'` check above the deny branch left **all 24 tests green**. The code was already correct; nothing pinned it. That is CLAUDE.md's Contract Enforcement rule verbatim: if a contract change can be made silently without a test failing, the contract is too weak. Three tests added - a paused deny, an expired deny, and a paused deny with a live allow beside it - and the mutation now fails (1 failed, 26 passed).
  - Notes (**I hand-rolled brand resolution that already existed**): the seed looked brands up with its own `Tenant.findOne` + `Brand.findOne` pair while `modules/tenancy/tenantResolver.ts` already exported `resolveBrandBySlug(tenantSlug, brandSlug)` - which had **zero callers**, which is how it stayed invisible. This is the same mistake that cost plan cycle 1 a 7/20: searching for the capability under the name I had just invented rather than searching for what the code does. The seed now delegates, with two tests asserting it passes **both** slugs (brand slugs are unique per tenant, so a one-argument lookup resolves something the schema does not guarantee). One accepted behaviour change: that function returns `null` on a database error as well as a genuine miss, so a lookup failure now reads as "brand absent" and skips - safe for a seed, self-healing next boot, and matching the resolver's documented fail-soft posture.
  - Notes (latent duplicate index, closed): `OfferFamily.slug` used column-level `unique: true`, which mints an index Sequelize names itself. If `sync({ alter: true })` ever ran, that would create a **second** unique index beside the DDL's `offer_families_slug_unique`. No global sync runs at boot, so it was latent - and latent is precisely how this repo acquired duplicate indexes before. Now declared in `indexes` with the DDL's own name, matching `BrandOfferPolicy`.
  - Notes (owed to T208, stated so "deferred" stays distinguishable from "forgotten"): `brand_offer_policies` and `offer_families` exist on dev1 but hold **0 rows** - the seed has never executed against a real database, because it only runs at backend boot. The wrong-slug risk that would silently leave a deny-everything table **is** retired: all four `(tenant_slug, brand_slug)` pairs were verified to resolve live. What remains unproven until a dev backend boots this code is the 24-row outcome.

This also covers a resolver **outage**, since `resolvePublicContext` is fail-soft and returns `unresolved` on a database error as readily as on a genuine miss - one behaviour for both, deliberately, so an outage cannot become an open door.
  - Notes (**a draft program is not a default - the third unenforced comment this phase**): T201's docstring claimed a mistakenly seeded program "cannot be resolved as a brand's default until someone activates it deliberately". That was a claim about resolution written before any resolution existed. T203 is where it becomes true, so `status === 'active'` is required and mutation-checked. This is now the **third** time this phase that a sentence I wrote in a comment turned out to be enforced by nothing - the T201 parity claim, the T202 "the two paths cannot disagree" claim, and this one. Treating every such sentence as a test I owe.
  - Notes (six no-program reasons stay distinct): `unresolved_context`, `brand_row_missing`, `brand_has_no_default`, `program_row_missing`, `program_not_active`, `lookup_failed`. A single `null` would leave an operator unable to tell "this brand has no journey yet" from "the programme is paused" from "the resolver is down", and those need different responses. One test asserts all six are reachable and distinct.
  - Notes (**the no-ALTER rule narrowed rather than vanished, and is pinned at a count of one**): T201/T202 asserted the module contains no `ALTER` at all, on the grounds that every column the run needed was on a table it creates itself. T203 genuinely reaches into `brands`, which `ensureMultiTenantSchema` owns. The assertion now permits exactly `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` and **excludes by name** `ALTER COLUMN`, `DROP COLUMN`, `SET DATA TYPE`, `SET NOT NULL`, `ADD CONSTRAINT` and `DROP CONSTRAINT` - plus the ALTER count is pinned at **one**, so a second one is a decision someone has to make deliberately rather than a line that slides in. Two further tests: the column must be nullable with no `DEFAULT` (either would rewrite every existing row - a locking migration, not an ensure step), and the FK must be `SET NULL`, since `CASCADE` would mean deleting a programme deleted its brand and every lead, domain and policy hanging off it.
  - Notes (**model parity, because Sequelize silently ignores what a model does not declare**): the column is added by the DDL but read through `Brand`, and Sequelize only ever touches attributes a model knows about - so omitting it would raise nothing, return `undefined` on read and drop writes silently. That exact failure already happened in this repo on the tenancy columns, and `ensureMultiTenantSchema.modelParity.test.ts` documents it; that test parses only **its own** statement list, so this module needed its own. A test parses the ALTER-added columns out of the SQL, asserts the pair is exactly `brands.default_journey_program_id` (non-vacuity - an empty parse would satisfy the loop), and checks the model declares it, nullable, referencing `journey_programs`.
  - Notes (**a mutation that SKIPPED silently, which is the finding worth keeping**): my first mutation run reported six killed and one "ANCHOR NOT UNIQUE (0) - SKIPPED" - and the skipped one was MUT-4, dropping the column from the `Brand` model, i.e. **the single most important check in the task**. The cause was CRLF: my anchor used `\n` against a file that uses `\r\n`. A skipped mutation looks almost exactly like a passed one in a summary line, which is how a vacuous assertion would have shipped wearing a mutation-tested badge. Re-run with the file's own line ending, as three variants: dropping the column, pointing it at the wrong table, and declaring it `NOT NULL` on the model. All three killed. **Nine of nine.**
  - Notes (live evidence, four controls): column **absent** before (0); `brands` went from **5 rows / 11 cols to 5 rows / 12 cols**, so exactly one column was added and **no row was rewritten**; pass 2 a no-op with the "already exists, skipping" notice; `nullable=YES`, `default=NONE`, `type=uuid`; the live FK reads `ON DELETE SET NULL`; **all 5 brands read NULL** rather than a guessed programme; and `explorer_%` unchanged at 86.
  - Notes (`legacy_host_map` is a defensive branch, and that is stated rather than hidden): `resolvePublicContext` emits only `source_slug`, `brand_domain` or `unresolved`, so this value is reachable **only with a stubbed resolver**. It is handled as a resolved path rather than falling into the unresolved branch, because the value is in the exported type union and a future resolver change that started emitting it must not silently return no programme for every visitor. Two tests, both honest about needing a stub.
  - Notes (three test bugs of mine, all found by running): `brand_domains` matched inside the **comment explaining that the module does not read it**, so the scan now strips comments first; `/DEFAULT/i` matched inside the column name `default_journey_program_id` - the same substring family as `toContain('id')` matching `tenant_id` - "fixed" with a word boundary that **was not actually on disk** - see the T203 re-grade note below, where four literal 0x08 bytes turned out to be sitting where the `\b` escapes were meant; and `Brand` is a **default** export, so `import { Brand }` was `undefined` and two tests died on `getAttributes` of undefined rather than on the thing they were testing.
  - Notes (**T203 attempt 1 FAILED at 9/12, criterion 6 = 0, and the finding was mine to own**): the independent verifier reproduced all 67 tests and all nine of my mutations as killed - then wrote three of its own that **survived**. Root cause: line 89 of the schema test contained **four literal 0x08 bytes where `\b` was meant**, written by an inline shell heredoc. Inside a regex, 0x08 looks exactly like a word boundary and silently disables the pattern, so the test named "the added column is NULLABLE and carries no DEFAULT" enforced only the NULLABLE half - a `DEFAULT gen_random_uuid()` passed **35 of 35**. The repo has a standing rule against writing source through a heredoc for precisely this failure, and a memory entry describing it by name. I broke it once, in one edit, and it produced a test that passed while enforcing nothing.
  - Notes (**and my rule was a blocklist, which can only refuse what somebody thought of**): the narrowed no-ALTER assertion excluded the reshaping forms by name - `ALTER COLUMN`, `DROP COLUMN`, `SET DATA TYPE`, `SET NOT NULL`, `ADD CONSTRAINT`, `DROP CONSTRAINT`. An appended `ADD UNIQUE (slug)` and `ADD CHECK (slug <> '')` both walked straight past it, because **Postgres does not require you to name a constraint to add one**. This was the AD-1 hard stop being loosened, which is the worst place to be approximately right. The rule is now an **allowlist**: every ALTER must match the whole permitted statement end to end, so anything appended to it or inserted inside it fails - including forms nobody has thought of. The named refusals are kept beside it, both because a named refusal tells the next person why and because it also covers a CREATE statement trying to smuggle one in, which an ALTER-shaped allowlist would never inspect.
  - Notes (**a control I added caught the allowlist being strict about the wrong thing**): X6 fed it an ALTER that was semantically the permitted shape with one extra space. It was **killed** - the pattern was matching literal single spaces between tokens. That is worse than it sounds: the next person to reformat this DDL gets a red test with no defect behind it, and the obvious way to make it green is to loosen the pattern, which is how an allowlist decays back into the blocklist it replaced. Now structurally strict and whitespace tolerant (`\s+` between every token), and X6 passes while X1-X4 stay killed.
  - Notes (a tripwire, so this cannot recur silently): a test now asserts the schema test file contains no 0x07, 0x08, 0x0b, 0x0c or 0x1b byte.   - Notes (**the programme is the switch, and it ships OFF - by design, not omission**): every programme is seeded `draft`, so `resolveDefaultJourney` returns `program_not_active` for every brand until a human activates one. T201 made `draft` the default precisely so a programme seeded by mistake cannot become a brand's default, and T203 enforces it; undoing either here would defeat both. Paths are seeded `active` instead, which looks inconsistent and is not: the programme gate already holds everything beneath it shut, so seeding paths draft too would mean five more deliberate actions per brand for no additional safety. A path's own status exists to retire ONE offer path later, which is a different decision.
  - Notes (**the paths are DERIVED, and that is what keeps §4 and §5 one system**): a programme's paths are exactly the families its brand is allowed, with denials subtracted, computed from T202's policy definitions. A literal path list per programme is how AI Flotation eventually acquires a `business_training` path that the runtime gate then refuses - a contradiction an operator reads as a policy bug rather than as two lists drifting. Asserted against **what the seed actually wrote**, not against the definitions, since the seed is the last place the derivation could go wrong.
  - Notes (**the slugs are exported because T205 will seed on the same brand**): `plan.md:191` has T205 registering Explorer as programme #1 on `colaberry-training`. If it writes its own slug literal there, that brand gets TWO programmes and the unique index on `(brand_id, slug)` does **not** catch it - two different slugs are two legal rows - and the brand default then points at whichever landed first. A duplicate the database is happy with is the worst kind. `PROGRAM_SLUGS` is exported and a test asserts every definition takes its slug from it, so T205 has one constant to import rather than a literal to invent.
  - Notes (**a mutation survived, and it was the same lesson I had already been taught one layer down**): deleting the denial subtraction from `allowedFamiliesFor` broke **no test** - because the four brands' allow and deny sets are disjoint today, so subtracting changes nothing. That makes this the **third** implementation of deny-outranks-allow, after `resolveOfferEligibility` and `allowedOfferFamilies`, and the second to ship unpinned; an independent review had caught exactly this shape in the second one and I did not carry it forward. Fixed by making the policy table **injectable** - the `lookupEntrySite` precedent in `orgAccountType.ts` - so a test can pass a contradictory pair the real definitions cannot express. M1 now fails, and a second mutation proves the injected table actually reaches the function rather than being ignored.

Not a finding - but it exposed a genuinely order-dependent assertion of mine (`pathPayloads().slice(0, 2)` assumed CPN was seeded first), now rewritten to assert the real property: each programme's paths numbered 0..n-1, no gaps, no duplicates, in any order. A rebuilt swap-based control passes.
  - Notes (a mock that hid a real design point): my brand mock returned the same `id` for every brand, and CPN and Colaberry Training deliberately **share** the slug `learner` - so their programme ids collided and two programmes' paths merged into one group. Per-brand slug uniqueness is exactly what keeps those two apart in the real schema, and the mock now reflects it.
  - Notes (it never overwrites a human): `status` is never written on an existing programme, and `brands.default_journey_program_id` is set **only when null** - not "only when unset to this programme". A human pointing a brand at a different programme has made a decision, and auto-correcting it on the next boot would be the override this seed is forbidden from doing. A test asserts the update payload contains that one key and nothing else.
  - Notes (nothing sends): these rows describe structure. No campaign row, no sequence enrolment, no message - and a path existing does not mean an offer may be made, because T202's eligibility gate is a separate check at the moment of use.

- [x] T204: the subject resolver, including the relationship half (AD-2)
  - Date: 2026-09-10
  - Session: CC-20260812-k4m9
  - What changed: `services/growthJourney/subjectResolver.ts` - a **read-only** subject view over the four identity tables (`leads`, `enrollments`, `visitors`, `org_members`) plus per-brand relationships from `lead_tenant_contexts` via `leadContextService.getLeadContexts`. Creates no table, writes no row.
  - Verification: 30 tests, **11 of 13 mutations killed with two controls passing** (the two survivors were both my own equivalent mutants - see below), `tsc --noEmit` exit 0.
  - Notes (**T204's contract as written was impossible, and this is the correction**): the plan says reuse `visitorTrackingService.resolveIdentity` AND "creates no table and writes no row". `resolveIdentity(visitorId, leadId): Promise<void>` **is not a resolver** - it writes `visitor.lead_id` plus the reverse link, returns nothing, and requires the caller to already know the lead id. Those two requirements cannot both hold, and it yields no identity in any case. That service exports **no** read-only identity function; all of `findOrCreateVisitor`, `getOrCreateSession`, `recordPageEvent`, `resolveIdentity` and `updateHeartbeat` write. So the visitor half reads the `Visitor` model directly, which is not reimplementing matching: the linking happens on the write path elsewhere, and reading an already-linked row is a query. Logged rather than quietly worked around, because "reuse the existing bridge" was a deliberate instruction and half of it was impossible as specified.
  - Notes (**the live footgun this task walked into**): `explorerIdentityBridge.resolveExplorerLead(enrollmentId, { dryRun })` **CREATES an `explorer_journey_profiles` row unless `dryRun` is set**. A subject resolver that forgot the flag would write a row per lookup on a page-view path - and **every read test would still pass**, because a passing read assertion does not notice a write. So `{ dryRun: true }` is passed on every call, and the no-write invariant is asserted in an `afterEach` that runs after **every** scenario rather than in one dedicated test: a single "it does not write" case only proves the one path it exercised. Eight writer spies (`create`/`update`/`upsert` on all four models) are checked each time. Dropping the flag, or passing `dryRun: false`, each fails two named assertions.
  - Notes (unresolved is not resolved-with-no-history): the result is a discriminated union. Somebody who exists but has no brand relationship and no visitor row is `resolved` with empty collections; somebody who could not be identified is `unresolved` with one of three reasons - `no_anchor_supplied`, `anchor_not_found`, `lookup_failed`. A single nullable return would collapse those, and "we could not identify this person" would start reading as "this person has no history", which is the difference between asking who someone is and deciding what to send them. A test asserts all three reasons are reachable and distinct; three mutations that collapse them are killed.
  - Notes (the relationship half is the part cycle 1 dropped): §15 names the deliverable a subject **and relationship** service, and plan cycle 1 omitted the second half - which is how a second per-brand relationship model gets built beside `lead_tenant_contexts` (19 rows). Relationships come back through `leadContextService`, and `subjectHasBrandRelationship` is derived from the **same** read rather than calling `hasBrandRelationship` separately, so the two answers cannot disagree and one lookup serves both. It also matches on tenant AND brand: matching brand alone is killed by a mutation.
  - Notes (**two of my own mutations were equivalent mutants, and I nearly read one as a finding**): M7 was `getLeadContexts(leadId + 0)` - semantically identical to the original, so its survival proved nothing. M7b added the table name inside a `//` comment, and the scan strips comments **by design** (that was the T203 fix, where scanning raw source flagged the module's own explanatory comment as the violation), so that survival is correct behaviour and "fixing" it would reintroduce the T203 bug. Third time this session I have written a mutation that changes no behaviour and then treated its survival as information. **A mutation that cannot fail is as useless as a test that cannot fail.**
  - Notes (what the bad mutation was hiding): the question M7 was meant to ask - what stops this module reading `lead_tenant_contexts` directly instead of through the service - had **no guard at all**. A spy on `getLeadContexts` proves the service was called; it cannot prove the table is not also read elsewhere. Added a source scan in the style of T203's no-new-map assertions, and two real mutations now die on it: importing `LeadTenantContext` directly, and swapping the service call for a `findAll`.
  - Notes (**graded 12/12 PASS; the verifier ran 27 mutations of its own and reproduced all ten of mine**): it confirmed both plan readings against source - `dryRun` suppresses every write in the bridge, and `resolveIdentity` performs **six** writes and returns void - and judged reading `Visitor` directly the right correction. Its own harness had a survivor that was its harness: `replace` on `{ dryRun: true }` hit my header comment first. It said so. Four residuals, all closed in the same pass: `email_normalized` carried the **raw** `Lead.email` on the lead-only path (no lowercase hook on the model, unique index on `LOWER(email)`, so mixed case exists) - now normalised through the bridge's own `normalizeEmail` so the two paths cannot disagree; two precedence branches were unpinned (caller `enrollmentId` over an org member's, caller `leadId` over the bridge's) - one test each; the cross-tenant confidentiality of `brand_relationships` is now stated **on the type** - `getLeadContexts` says callers MUST filter by authorized tenants, nothing crosses HTTP with that field today, and the type now names `getAuthorizedLeadContexts` as the filter any future route must apply; redundant casts removed and the log line gained `service`/`timestamp`. Suite 30 → 33.

  - Notes (**one of T205's acceptance criteria is unsound, and one I WRONGLY called unsound - retracted**): (1) I first wrote that "the 14 seam characterization tests" were "defined nowhere - not in discovery, not in the contract, and no characterization file exists", and that claim went into commit `cc75d918`'s message. **It is false on every count**, and the T205 verifier found it: `execution-contract.md:62-63` defines them ("the 14 characterization tests written in Phase 0" - my grep missed it because the number and the noun are on different lines), and the file EXISTS - `explorerPipelineSeams.characterization.test.ts`, commit `197259f9`, on `feat/explorer-fact-resolver` (the PR #2346 branch), which is **not an ancestor of this workstream**. (2) "All 217 profiles resolve a program; run against `accelerator_dev1` first" - **dev1 holds ZERO `explorer_journey_profiles`**; the 217 are in production. That criterion cannot be satisfied on dev1 at all, not for want of a deploy but because the data is not there. Neither is dropped and neither is claimed. The 217-row backfill can only be verified in production, which is T208's job behind the deploy gate.
  - Notes (**`subject_ref` is the price of AD-2**): there is no `subjects` table, so a participation row cannot point at a subject id - none exists. It carries a derived key instead, `enrollment:<uuid>` or `lead:<id>`, built in ONE exported function so the backfill and any future writer cannot disagree about what identifies a person; two spellings of the same person would each get a row and the unique index would be satisfied by both. **Enrollment wins over lead deliberately**: an enrollment id is stable for the enrollment's life, while the lead a profile resolves to can change as identities merge - keying on the less stable anchor would hand the same person a second participation row after a merge. And `subjectRef({ leadId: 0 })` returns `lead:0`, not null: `0` is falsy, and a truthiness check there silently drops a real lead.
  - Notes (**idempotent by database index, not by intention**): the unique index on `(program_id, subject_ref)` is what makes running this twice over 217 profiles produce 217 rows rather than 434 - and that holds under a **concurrent** second run, which an application-level "already done?" check would not. A unique violation on insert is counted as `enrollments_raced`, not failed, because it is the index doing its job. The raw Postgres code `23505` is recognised as well as the Sequelize class name.
  - Notes (**it refuses rather than creating its destination**): if T212's programme is missing the backfill stops with `refused: 'program_not_found'` and does not even read the profiles. A backfill that creates its own programme cannot tell "the seed has not run" from "the seed ran and I disagreed with it". It imports `PROGRAM_SLUGS.colaberryTraining` - the forward obligation that constant was built for two tasks ago - so this task cannot produce the second-programme duplicate the `(brand_id, slug)` index would not catch.
  - Notes (the path is left null, deliberately): a participation row may name a `journey_paths` row and this backfill does not. An Explorer profile carries no offer-family signal, so choosing one would be inventing an offer for 217 real people - and §4's eligibility gate would then be asked about a path nobody chose.
  - Notes (**reconciliation counts the destination, and knows when it is counting nothing**): `reconcileExplorerBackfill` counts `growth_journey_enrollments` rows on the programme against `explorer_journey_profiles`, because "the backfill reported 217 created" and "217 people are on the programme" are different claims - this run has already shipped one nightly job that reported `succeeded: 152, failed: 0` while 60 learners were invisible to it. It also returns `vacuous: true` when there were no profiles to map, because on dev1 it would otherwise report `0 === 0, matched: true` having back-filled nobody - the precise shape of vacuous pass this programme keeps being bitten by. A caller must treat `vacuous` as "not yet proven".
  - Notes (my own additive guards refused this task's table, which is them working): adding `growth_journey_enrollments` failed two tests - the Y1 index guard and the declared-table list - until the fifth table was declared explicitly. Adding a table is now a deliberate edit rather than a line that slides in. `enrollment_id` is asserted NOT to be a foreign key, so the absence reads as a decision rather than an oversight.
  - Files: `backend/src/db/ensureGrowthJourneySchema.ts`, `backend/src/db/__tests__/ensureGrowthJourneySchema.test.ts`, `backend/src/models/GrowthJourneyEnrollment.ts`, `backend/src/models/index.ts`, `backend/src/services/growthJourney/explorerProgramBridge.ts`, `backend/src/services/growthJourney/__tests__/explorerProgramBridge.test.ts`

Handled at the route with **refuse, never widen**: a requested tenant or brand that did not come back granted is a 403 before any lookup, because proceeding would hand a brand-scoped operator their whole tenant for typing the wrong id. Documented in the bridge rather than changed in the builder that every guard depends on - that change is a security-module decision with a blast radius across the system, and it is recorded for the handoff instead.

Fail-closed. `enterpriseIntelligenceController` uses a "be generous while the membership system is unpopulated" fallback for its own reads; that is **deliberately not adopted** for a brand-scoped route. The **entire reason** the guard returns 404 rather than 403 is to not confirm the row exists - and the body confirmed it, thirty lines below the comment stating the rule. My "indistinguishable by design" test compared status only. Fixed: a 404 from the guard now renders the byte-identical `{ error: 'Not found' }`, the class is logged server-side where it belongs, and the test compares **whole bodies** and asserts no `error_class` or `TenantIsolation` text. A 403 keeps its class - by then the caller already knows the row exists. Leaking it back is killed by two tests.
  - Notes (**G2 - brand confinement is opt-in, and it is the larger hole**): the tenancy builder never **derives** a brand scope from a brand-restricted membership; it only validates one the caller requests. So a Training-only operator who omits `?brand_id=` reads an Enterprise row with 200, and refuse-never-widen cannot fire because nothing was requested. The route cannot close this - the context carries the granted brand, not the membership's. The fix is in `buildRequestContext` (auto-confine when every membership in the operating tenant shares one non-null `brand_id`, mirroring the single-tenant auto-select four lines above it) and that is a security-module change kept out of this run deliberately. **Pinned by a test named as a known gap**, asserting today's 200, with a comment saying that when the builder is fixed this test should fail and that failure is the signal to flip it to 403. Named for Ali's decision in the handoff beside G1.
  - Notes (flag-gated now, matching the plan's rollback text): at `08bfe3f8` the route read no flag because T209's module did not exist yet - the plan's "parallel, independent" edge made that unavoidable. Now gated on the **master** (`GROWTH_JOURNEY_ENABLED`), 404 when off so the route's existence is not revealed, after `requireAdmin` so an unauthenticated caller still sees 401. Reads the master only; the dark-launch guard forbids a sub-flag read anywhere else, and the route test derives the three names from the module's keys at runtime rather than writing them - because writing them tripped that guard.
  - Notes (two operational facts the verifier confirmed for T208): `{ tenant_id: null }` matches nothing **on this table** because `growth_journey_enrollments.tenant_id` is `NOT NULL` - the property is table-specific and would be false on a table with legacy NULL tenant rows. - [x] T301: the decision, profile and snapshot tables - the three Phase 3 tables Phase 2 deferred
  - Date: 2026-09-12

Two of those fields have no source anywhere in this codebase - nothing records whether a human is already in conversation with someone, and there is no sales-capacity table at all - so `human_conversation` and `sales_capacity` default to `'unknown'` and the row says so rather than omitting the question. It is keyed on a hashed `idempotency_key`, NOT on Explorer's `(enrollment_id, decision_date)`: that key cannot express one decision per subject per BRAND, and Explorer's `enrollment_id` is NOT NULL with a foreign key to `enrollments`, so a business subject physically cannot be recorded there. `growth_journey_profiles` (18 columns) is the ONE mutable table this run owns - a projection of where a subject currently stands, unique per `(brand_id, subject_ref)` so one person can stand in a different place in each brand - and every state change it records also writes an append-only transitions row of the new `state_changed` type through Phase 2's existing writer. `growth_journey_score_snapshots` (10 columns) is append-only and unique per `(brand_id, subject_ref, as_of_date)`, closing a gap in Explorer's own snapshots table, which has no unique index at all and silently doubles its history on a second run that day. Three models with both-directions DDL-to-model parity; `classification_id` is deliberately not a foreign key, so a decision survives its classification being superseded.
